# Environment

- Personal setup spans machines: a Linux host (`~`) that runs the services (kanban board app, node-agent fleet under pm2, Tailscale), plus a real Mac used as the hands-on dev machine. Reach the Mac with `ssh mac-tailscale` (Tailscale, BatchMode) to inspect actual workspace/git state. Confidence: 0.85
- The host fleet is three machines: this Linux VPS (x86_64), the Mac (`ssh mac-tailscale`, darwin arm64), and a Windows box (`ssh windows-tailscale`, win32 x64 — drive it with `powershell -NoProfile -Command`, and escape `\$env:` so the outer shell doesn't expand it away). An install ask that names them ("in this vm, local mac and local windows") means all three, each verified by running the binary on that host — the VPS is the control plane, the Mac and Windows are the worker hosts that actually exec the CLI, so an install landing only on the box you're logged into is half the task. The Windows box also ships a bundled `bun` 1.3.13 (Kiro-Cli) one patch below what some installers gate on, which makes a default install silently fall into a source build and fail. Confidence: 0.8
- **The agent-profile `active` flag is a real persisted pointer now, not a hardcoded lie** — `internal/kanban/active_profile.go` stores `~/.hermes/active-profile.json` (`{"profile":"<name>"}`) via `hermesHome()`, sitting beside `executors.json` / `jev-routing.json` rather than in hermes's own `config.yaml`, and deliberately *not* in `~/.hermes/profile.yaml` which hermes owns (it holds `description`). `ActiveProfile()` / `SetActiveProfile(name)` / `DefaultProfile(name)` are the whole surface: the getter falls back to `default` on a missing file, corrupt JSON, or a name that no longer exists on disk; the setter **refuses** an unknown name rather than persisting it. `POST /api/profiles/{name}/activate` is the route (`ProfilesPage.tsx:452` had called it all along against a 404). Before this, `profile.go` and `ListProfiles` both hardcoded `Active: true` for `default` and nothing consumed it. Default resolution now routes through `DefaultProfile` at: new chat sessions (`chat.go`), the workspace fork (`chat_workspace.go`), the notification read/record pair (`notifications.go` — both sides must agree, they scope by profile), the frontend chat/memory pickers, and `profileQuery` (MCP/extension config). Tests are `active_profile_test.go` using `t.Setenv("HERMES_HOME", t.TempDir())` with a `writeProfileDir` helper. Confidence: 0.85
- Board auth is **single shared password, no identity record**: `internal/kanban/auth.go` stores one `salt` + `password_hash` in `auth_config`, `ValidateSession` returns a bare `bool`, and `/api/auth/status` returns only `{authenticated}`. There is no user/display-name/role to read, so "wire the avatar to the real user" is not implementable — don't invent a plausible-looking identity. The honest resolution is a neutral affordance (e.g. a `UserRound` icon button labelled "Signed in") with optional `name`/`role` props left for when a user table exists. Confidence: 0.7
- **That identity gap has since been closed as *workspace* identity, not user identity — and the distinction is the whole design.** `internal/kanban/workspace_identity.go` stores `~/.hermes/workspace-identity.yaml` (`name` + `avatar_url`) via `hermesHome()`, plus `workspace-avatar` (blob) and `workspace-avatar.json` (sniffed mime), with `SaveWorkspaceIdentity` atomic and `SetWorkspaceName` as the name-only partial update. `GET/PUT /api/workspace`, `GET /api/workspace/avatar`, `PUT /api/workspace/avatar-url`, `POST/DELETE /api/workspace/avatar` are registered in `cmd/server/main.go` next to `/api/settings/*`, so they inherit the `authHandler` `/api/` prefix gate. Front end: `web/src/features/settings/tabs/WorkspaceTab.tsx` owns the query (`["workspace-identity"]`, `staleTime: 60_000`), the `WORKSPACE_NAME_FALLBACK = "Switchyard"` constant, `workspaceMonogram()`, and `workspaceAvatarSrc()`; `web/src/components/nav-user.tsx` imports all three from that one module so the top-bar trigger, the account menu and the settings preview can't drift. **"Profile" in this product means an *agent* profile (`~/.hermes/profiles/*/config.yaml`) — never conflate it with the workspace record when reading a request that says "change my profile".** Confidence: 0.85
- **The agent-profile avatar API (`GET/POST/DELETE /api/profiles/{name}/avatar`, `PUT .../avatar-url`) has always existed and `ProfilesPage.tsx` already has upload/URL-paste/remove with cache-busting** — so "add avatar editing" is usually already half-built and the real gap is the *identity* concept, not the upload. Check for the existing endpoint before designing one. Confidence: 0.8
- **Frontend gaps that cost nothing to fix but nothing consumes**: chat organisation (projects/tags/pinning/lineage) is fully implemented server-side with `api.ts` helpers that **nothing imports**; `retryChatRun` exists with no button; board export/import is implemented and unreachable. A missing-route call (`POST /api/profiles/{name}/activate` from `ProfilesPage.tsx:452` — **no such route existed in the Go tree** until this pass added it, see the active-flag bullet above; the other examples — chat organisation helpers in `api.ts` that nothing imports, `retryChatRun` with no button, board export/import implemented and unreachable — remain) and polling settings that are theatre (`BoardPage.tsx:144` hardcodes 10s, `OutputPanels.tsx:530` polls every 1200ms, `WorkspacesPage.tsx:651` fires one request per workspace every 5s, while a working SSE stream sits unused) are the same class: capability present, consumer absent. An audit pass is the thing that finds these; check "is this helper imported anywhere?" before calling a feature missing. Confidence: 0.8
- **`TaskEvents` returns `ORDER BY id DESC` (`kanban.go:437`) and `groupRuns` walks the slice forward (`switchyard_backend.go:169`), so every task's run panel reads backwards** — wrong attempt counts, wrong order, phantom runs. Existing tests feed ASC literals directly and never touch the real path, which is why it survived. A **reverse-ordered query paired with a forward-walking reducer** is a class of bug no type system catches; when a report says "the panel shows the wrong thing", compare the query's ORDER BY against the reducer's loop direction before assuming the data is bad. Confidence: 0.8
- **Double-firing one endpoint from two call sites in the same chain trips its own idempotency guard and surfaces as a false error.** `TaskDetailPage.tsx:433` calls `cancelRun` then `onStop`, which resolves to the *same* endpoint (`App.tsx:222`); the second call hits `status == "running"` → 400, `onSuccess` never runs, and a task that **did** stop shows a red error. Trace `onStop`/`onCancel` props all the way to their definition before assuming a button is wired to one thing. Confidence: 0.8
- **Drag-and-drop reorder posts only the destination column while the server appends everything it wasn't given** — `position` is one global sequence, so a within-column drag pushes the other column's cards to the tail, an active filter shoves every hidden card behind, and a `fetch` fires from inside a state updater. Any "send me the full ordering" API has to be sent the full ordering, including the filtered-out rows. Confidence: 0.75
- A task's workspace often lives only on the Mac while the board is served from the Linux host, so the board-side view of a task can be empty or wrong. Check the remote workspace before concluding there is nothing to review. Confidence: 0.75
- Tasks are addressed as `board/<board-slug>/task/<task-id>`. Board data lives in `~/.hermes/kanban/boards/<slug>/kanban.db`, but `boardDir("default")` resolves to hermesHome() itself — the real default board is `~/.hermes/kanban.db` (~20MB, fully migrated). The `~/.hermes/kanban/boards/default/` dir holds only a 4KB uninitialized stub, and hand-writing tables into it wastes a long detour: nothing the dispatcher polls will ever see them. Locate the DB the server actually reads before touching it. Confidence: 0.85
- A task seeded with an empty `assignee` is rejected by the dispatcher's model gate with `profile "" not found` — profiles are filesystem-based (`~/.hermes/profiles/*/config.yaml`) and resolved by name via `ProfileModel`. When creating a card directly, always set a valid assignee/profile or the card sits unclaimed. Confidence: 0.8
- The kanban-board HTTP API requires a session cookie ("authentication required"), so `/api/nodes` and friends can't be curl'd without login. Drive card-level work through the board's sqlite file plus `~/.pm2/logs/kanban-board-{out,error}.log`, which is where dispatch attempts, `continuation=true`, and `session_id=…` decisions get logged. Confidence: 0.75
- **The login is a one-line `curl` and the cookie it returns is the key to everything else.** `curl -s -c /tmp/ck.txt -X POST http://localhost:8790/api/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"password":"…"}'` writes a Netscape cookie jar; the session cookie is named **`kanban_session`**, and from then on every probe takes `-b /tmp/ck.txt` (`GET /api/auth/status` → `{"authenticated":true}`, `GET /api/flow/active` → the live map payload). `curl -c` alone does **not** print the value, so the follow-up `curl -i … | grep -i '^set-cookie'` or `grep -oE 'kanban_session\s+\S+' /tmp/ck.txt | awk '{print $2}'` is what extracts it for a browser script. The live `:8790` instance's password is **not** the seeded `123456` (that returns `{"error":"invalid password"}`) — the user supplies it, so don't retry variants or reset it; that single fact is worth asking for once and reusing the whole session afterwards. Pair it with the screenshot-workflow note in tooling: this cookie is exactly what makes a rendered capture possible without a login flow in the browser. Confidence: 0.8
- node-agent ships **two** binaries that must be rebuilt and restarted together: `node-agent-server` (long-running, holds the :8788 HTTP API and decodes dispatch JSON) and `agent` (the per-node worker). Restarting only the agent leaves a stale server that *silently drops* newly added `internal/transport` JSON fields — the worker never learns the session id and quietly cold-starts instead of resuming. Cross-process version skew is invisible until a card is created. Confidence: 0.8
- The worker does not pass `--model`, so a board-level `model` field is ignored for this executor and omp resolves the model from the host's own config — a second, silent reason a card can be selectable and dispatchable and still not do what the board says. omp also keeps its **provider credentials in its own store** (`~/.omp/agent/`, `omp setup`), never in the node-agent environment, so a host with working `dsh` and `codex` can still have nothing usable for omp: the Mac's `com.adit.node-agent.plist` carries only `NODE_AGENT_ID`/`SERVER`/`TOKEN`/`SHELL_*` and no provider key, and the failure surfaces late — after the executor is resolved, the binary is found, and argv is built — as `No models available. Use /login or set an API key environment variable.` Installing the CLI is therefore not the same as making the executor work, and the second half is a per-host user step. Confidence: 0.75
- Local service repos live under `~/apps/*` and are Go backends (`kanban-board`, `node-agent`) with web frontends. Confidence: 0.65
- `/tmp` on the VPS is **not durable** — files scp'd into it were gone by the next command, so intermediate artifacts that must survive a round-trip belong in `$COMMANDCODE_SCRATCHPAD` (the path is already exported), not in `/tmp`. Copy to a remote host's `/tmp`, do the install, and `rm -f` the copies there in the same command so nothing is left behind.
- In `kanban-board`, **`tasks.status` is the dispatcher's claim channel, not a free-form label** — the full set is `triage/todo/scheduled/ready/running/blocked/review/done/archived`, and the remote dispatcher picks up `ready`/`running` and spawns a worker for them, so a new subsystem that flips a card to `running` has silently re-triggered the coding task. Any auxiliary lifecycle (e2e runs, hooks, sweeps) needs its **own table plus its own event kind** — `insertEventTx(tx, taskID, kind, payload)` takes arbitrary kinds, which slots in with zero migration and is already swept by `ClearExecutionHistory`. `DispatchRemoteRaw` in `internal/kanban/nodeagent.go` passes `persistTask=false`, which skips `ClaimTaskRun` entirely: no status write, no dispatcher interference, no run-fence needed — that is the seam for work that runs on a workspace host without owning a task. The server never runs an executor locally; everything goes out over the node-agent HTTP channel. Also note `AddComment` (`comments.go:112`) auto-requeues a `review` task back to `todo`, so machine-written results on a card must use their own transaction, never the comment API. Column changes must also be threaded through `taskSelectCols`/`scanTask` **and** `taskByID`'s own hardcoded list, which already silently omits `execution_meta`. Confidence: 0.75
- `review → done` is reachable from **two** independent server paths, and only one of them is the approval endpoint: `cmd/server/review.go`'s `/approve` handler, and the generic `PATCH /api/boards/{slug}/tasks/{id}/status` in `cmd/server/main.go`, which calls `reviewWorkspaceClean` and then runs `StatusTransition` on its own. A guard written only into `/approve` is bypassable by dragging the card, so any blocking rule has to be applied at both call sites. Confidence: 0.8
- A new user-editable config store in this repo **mirrors `internal/kanban/executor_settings.go` exactly**: `~/.hermes/<feature>.json` via `hermesHome()`, the `Default*`/`Normalize*`/`Load*`/`Save*` quadruple, `GET` + `PUT /api/settings/<feature>` in a `cmd/server/<feature>_routes.go` registered from `main.go`, and a panel in `web/src/features/settings/` composed into `SettingsPage.tsx`. The e2e profiles landed as `e2e_settings.go` + `E2ESettings.tsx` added *inside* the existing `executors` tab rather than as a new one, with the task dialog seeding its picker from `settings.default` — and the server's normalizer guaranteeing that default can never point at a removed profile, so a hand-edited file can't leave the picker seeding a phantom suite. Confidence: 0.75
- The `kanban-board` web app has **no jsdom**: component tests render with `renderToStaticMarkup`, so conditional UI (e.g. whether a button shows for a status) must be extracted into a pure exported helper like `shouldShowE2EButton(status)` to be testable. Both halves are needed: the pure helper pins the rule, and a `renderToStaticMarkup(<TaskCard task={…} onRunE2E={() => {}} />)` case driven by a real card's field values is what proves the button is in the markup and not `disabled` — the repo's no-jsdom renderer is the verification vehicle, not just a test harness. **Anything inside a Radix Portal renders as nothing under `renderToStaticMarkup`** (a `Dialog`'s children simply don't appear), so a component whose only test vehicle is that renderer is untestable as written — export the meaningful inner piece (`ViewerToolbar`, the dialog body) and assert that directly rather than shipping a test that proves nothing or adding a test-env dependency. Per-card action buttons are gated by the `STATUS_TARGETS` map in `web/src/features/board/TaskCard.tsx` — `done` currently maps to `[]`, so a `done`-only footer renders nothing at all. `WorkerLogPanel` already holds the repo's 1.2s incremental-tail polling pattern for long-running output. The backend's attachment MIME allowlist rejects `application/json` and `text/plain`, so attaching a machine report needs the allowlist widened first. Confidence: 0.7
- node-agent `/api/dispatch` routes by exact `workspace` match against each node's registered list (the Mac keeps them in `~/.hermes/workspaces.json`) and silently falls back to an arbitrary non-offline node on a miss — so an "empty/no changes" review often means "dispatched to the wrong machine", not a clean workspace. Matching is by **path prefix**, not equality: the `cc-win-e2e` card's `C:\Development\Next\next-portfolio-blog` sits under the registered root `C:\Development` and routes to the `windows` node correctly (verified by dispatch + result), so a miss is a genuinely unregistered root, not a deeper-directory miss. Confidence: 0.75
- **The worker refuses a workspace directory that does not exist, so any control-plane feature that dispatches into a path it must first create has a hard ordering requirement.** `cmd/agent/main.go` (around line 543) stats the requested workspace and fails with `workspace not found` before running anything, and the "routes by workspace prefix" claim in the control plane is segment-aware (`workspaceOwnedBy`, `cmd/server/main.go:507`), so `<repo>/.switchyard/<task-id>` still routes to the node owning `<repo>`. Those two facts together are why git-worktree-per-task needed **no node-agent change at all**: create the directory over the existing shell dispatch *before* the agent runs. A worker-side "does this path exist" check is the constraint to look for in any remote-execution design, because it silently converts "the control plane made the path" into "the worker did". Confidence: 0.85
- **There are two distinct workspace guards in the remote-execution path, and conflating them produces a test that asserts the wrong failure.** (1) The gateway rejects an *unregistered* workspace with **409 `no node owns workspace`** before it is ever queued — a node claims workspaces by path prefix, so an unknown path has no owner and never reaches the worker. (2) A workspace that *is* registered but whose directory doesn't exist is rejected by the worker itself with **`workspace not found`**, when it tries to run the command. A first test that conflated them failed with `no node owns workspace` where it expected `workspace not found`; the corrected version asserts both, in order. The practical split: routing only requires the path to sit under a registered root (which is why `<repo>/.switchyard/<id>` routes correctly the moment it exists as a prefix), while the existence check is the one that makes worktree creation ordering load-bearing. Confidence: 0.85
- The architecture is a **central router plus per-host agents**, and that split decides what a connection error means: `node-agent-server` runs on the VPS holding :8788 and routes to the `agent` worker on each host. So `dial tcp 127.0.0.1:8788: connect: connection refused` is always **the central router being down** — never a per-host problem, and never something to fix by touching the Mac or Windows box. Check `ss -ltnp | grep 8788`, then `./ctl.sh status` and `./ctl.sh start`; both nodes re-registered within seconds of the start and their `/health` entries went `idle` with `shell` advertised. Health is readable without a session by hitting the node-agent API directly with the token: `curl -H "X-Node-Agent-Token: $(grep NODE_AGENT_TOKEN ~/.hermes/node-agent.env | cut -d= -f2)" http://127.0.0.1:8788/health`. Confidence: 0.8
- **Tailscale hostnames do not resolve from the VPS even when the host is online.** `getent hosts windows-tailscale` returned nothing while `tailscale status` listed the machine with a real address (windows `100.122.101.28`, mac `100.75.2.78`) — and `mac-tailscale` resolved while `windows-tailscale` did not, so it is not a blanket DNS failure. Resolve the address out of `tailscale status` and probe that, rather than concluding a host is down from a failed `getent` or from `ssh` refusing the alias. Confidence: 0.75
- node-agent's result payload is **invalid JSON for Windows jobs**: `output` carries raw Windows paths (`C:\Users\User\AppData\Local\npm-cache\…`) with unescaped backslashes, so a client-side `json.Unmarshal` fails on a run that actually succeeded. Read the `/api/results/<id>` body raw (and un-escape the `\r\n` yourself) when you need the output off a Windows node, and say so in the report rather than presenting it as a parse bug in your own code. Confidence: 0.75
- The kanban dashboard is behind a password the agent doesn't have, so it cannot click through the UI — verify card behaviour via the same API/command path the card uses and say so explicitly rather than claiming the UI works. Confidence: 0.7
- **A rendered verification of the app should run an isolated instance, never the user's live one.** `HERMES_HOME=$COMMANDCODE_SCRATCHPAD/hermes KANBAN_ADDR=127.0.0.1:8799 go run ./cmd/server` gives a fresh board DB seeded with password `123456` and touches none of the real `~/.hermes`; the long-running instance on 8790 has had its password changed, so `123456` is rejected there and the shell never mounts. Three facts this cost time to learn: the Go toolchain **is** present on this VPS (the "no Go" note is about the Windows box), the Go server serves both the API and the built SPA, and a static server in front of `dist` (`npx serve -s dist`) returns `index.html` for `/api/*` — so login posts to nothing and the failure looks like a rendering bug, not a missing backend. Stop what you started and confirm the ports are released, while leaving the pre-existing 8790 instance up and untouched. Confidence: 0.85
- `pgrep -f <pattern>` / `ps ... | grep` run inside a `shell_command` will match the wrapper shell carrying the same pattern text, so it reports a bogus "stray process" and a `kill` on that PID hits the agent's own shell rather than the target. Two bites in one session on the node-agent server, and five more at the end of the screenshot pass — where the *shell's own command line* contained the literal pattern text, so `pgrep -f chrome-headless-shell` reported "chrome STILL" three times in a row even after `pkill -9` had already killed everything, and even sent `pkill -9 -f "vite"` at the wrapper. The fix that settles it: anchor on the **full path** (`pgrep -a -f 'chrome-headless-shell-linux64/chrome-headless-shell'`) or read `ps -eo pid,comm,args --no-headers | grep -E '<pattern>' | grep -v grep`, and confirm what a PID actually is before killing it. Corroborate with the thing that can't lie about its own name — `curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" --max-time 3 http://localhost:5173/` for a dev server, a listening-port check for a socket — because a process list that includes your own query is not evidence a process survives. Confidence: 0.85
- Boards and node agents persist state under `~/.hermes/`; config tokens in `~/.hermes/node-agent.env`, service logs in `~/.node-agent/` and `~/.pm2/logs/`. Confidence: 0.65
- Deploy loop: kanban-board → `go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server` then `pm2 restart kanban-board` (VPS) — a plain `kill <pid>` is enough too, because the supervisor respawns it within a second (it came back as a new pid unprompted), but discover the launch mechanism from `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` plus the process env first so the restart matches how it was started. There is **no build script at all** in this repo — the build line above is typed by hand every time, which is what makes stale-binary bugs recur. `./ctl.sh` has `build`, `build-mac`, and `build-windows` (the latter two cross-compile into `dist/`), and the server's `/dl` endpoint reads `dist/` **on every request** — so worker binaries can be rebuilt and redeployed without touching the running server, while the server binary itself still needs `./ctl.sh build` plus a restart. Caveat: `./ctl.sh status` reported the server stopped while PID 576626 was in fact serving :8788/:8789 (it was never in ctl.sh's pidfile), so confirm the process with `ss -ltnp` and `/proc/<pid>/cwd` before trusting either the status output or a `kill`. node-agent → `./ctl.sh build` then `./ctl.sh restart`, or for a live E2E `go build -o node-agent-server ./cmd/server` and `go build -o agent ./cmd/agent` then restart **both**. An agent can also run ad hoc against the local server with `NODE_AGENT_SERVER=http://127.0.0.1:8788 NODE_AGENT_ID=<id> NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=$(grep -oP '(?<=^NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=).*' ~/.hermes/node-agent.env) ./agent`. On the Mac the node-agent is a launchd agent `com.adit.node-agent` with KeepAlive, so `launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.adit.node-agent` restarts it and forces workspace re-registration. Confidence: 0.8
- **A pm2 deploy is scoped to the named app, and it isn't done until the *served* app answers.** The sequence that settles a "build it and start pm2" ask: `pm2 list` → `pm2 describe <app>` (script path, exec cwd, which binary it runs) → `pm2 env <id>` to read what the process actually has — which is how you learn **no env vars need passing**, because a bare `pm2 restart <name>` inherits the app's stored environment rather than the shell's → build (`go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server`) → `pm2 restart <app>`. Restarting only the named entry matters: this box also runs `9router` and other entries unrelated to the deploy, so `pm2 restart all` would bounce services you were never asked to touch — name the ones you left running in the close-out. The gate is live HTTP rather than the supervisor's status line: `/` → 200 whose HTML references the **new** hashed assets (`index-<hash>.js` / `index-<hash>.css`), an `/api/…` route → 401 (session gate up, route exists), and `pm2 logs <app> --lines 30 --nostream` for a clean startup. Quote the pid, the port and the asset hashes. Two additions from the `start pm2` pass: the documented **rollout order in README's deploy section** (`go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server` then `pnpm build` in `web/`) is the authoritative build sequence — read it rather than reconstructing one; and this box's `9router` gateway was **already stopped** alongside every other pm2 service, with the README flagging it as never-stop, so surface that as pre-existing state needing a separate start instead of quietly leaving the gateway down or bouncing it unasked. Confidence: 0.8
- `~/.hermes/workspaces.json` exists in two places (VPS and Mac) and can drift: the Mac's copy is what node-agent actually routes on, so sync it rather than trusting the VPS copy. Confidence: 0.75
- node-agent prefixes every result `output` with a `provenance executor=... args=[...]` line that echoes the *entire* command, including any literal sentinel strings the command contains (e.g. `printf '__STAT__\n'`). Any parser that finds its marker with `strings.Index`/first-occurrence will parse that echo instead of the real output — anchor sentinels on whole lines and skip the preamble. Confidence: 0.75
- VPS↔Mac traffic runs over a Tailscale **DERP relay (DERP(sin)), not a direct path** — ~32ms ping, ~65ms per HTTP round trip, so every blocking agent→server call has real cost. Check `tailscale ping` / `tailscale netcheck` before blaming code for latency. Confidence: 0.7
- node-agent per shell job used to pay ~7 blocking `postJSON` round trips (4 `emit` progress events + busy/idle heartbeats + result) plus a mandatory 400ms wait in `streamCommand` (loop exits only when `pending == nil`, cleared solely by the 400ms ticker): ~1.65s wall for ~60-80ms of real git work. Fixed via an ordered async `eventEmitter` (queue + single drainer, `Close()` drains before the result post, events batched not dropped) and exiting the stream loop on closure with a synchronous final flush — now ~1.0s. The residual ~1s is the DERP relay, not code. Confidence: 0.75
- The VPS root disk routinely sits at **100%** (300-400 MB free) on a volume that measured ~20GB in an early session and **~40G** at a later audit, which blocks cross-compiles. `go clean -cache` frees the ~1.2GB `~/.cache/go-build` and the build then works, but the cache refills on the next build, so check `df -h /` *before* a cross-compile and report the headroom again afterwards — a build that "succeeded" may leave the box one command away from failing. Confidence: 0.7
- **Disk-space audit recipe for the VPS.** "Why is storage almost full" is answered with evidence, not a guess: `df -h / /home` → `du -xh -d1` top level → drill into the largest dir → reconcile `du -xsh /` against `df` (they matched at 36G vs 37G, i.e. no leak) → then hunt reclaimable candidates by class. Use `-x` throughout so other mounts don't distort the numbers, and pipe through `sort -rh | head -N` to keep output readable. Two probes worth running every time: `lsof -nP | awk '/deleted/ {s+=$8}'` for **deleted-but-open files** (the usual "mystery space" culprit — here 0.5 MB total, proving the space is real), and `sudo -n tune2fs -l /dev/vda2 | grep -i reserved` because **ext4 reserves ~5% for root** (445,577 blocks ≈ 1.7G here), which is why the user sees far less free space than the totals imply. Reclaimable classes to check, in order: `~/.cache/go-build` (220M, regenerable and it refills), stale `node_modules` for apps absent from `pm2 list`, stale `.next`/`dist`/`.turbo` build outputs, deleted-but-still-registered git worktrees (`git worktree list --porcelain | grep -B1 prunable` — a *prunable* entry whose directory is already gone costs only metadata), npm/pnpm caches, and a full `.git` in an install-only clone. Confidence: 0.75
- **Disk-heavy locations on the VPS** (measured at the 40G audit): `~/.hermes` **7.7G** is the single biggest tree — of which `hermes-agent/.git` is **1.4G** (a full clone of `NousResearch/hermes-agent` kept only so the agent can be updated; the largest single reclaimable item, and any change to it touches the *running* agent), `hermes-agent/tools/` is **1.6G** (ffmpeg 512M, python 401M, chromium 364M, node 227M — in use, don't touch), and `hermes-agent/venv` + `.venv` are **813M** for what looks like a duplicate pair in one repo. Elsewhere: `~/apps` 6.8G (mostly `node_modules` — next-portfolio-blog 1.5G, cpns 1.1G, habbit 793M — plus `.next` at 553M and 410M). **Caveat learned during the cleanup: `next-portfolio-blog` is LIVE under pm2** (`pm2 describe` shows `next` running with its exec cwd there, and its 410M `.next` *is* the serving build) — never treat "stale `.next` build output" as a free win without a per-app liveness check; only `cpns-preparation/.next` (553M) turned out to be genuinely dead. The same repo on the **Mac** is the opposite state — no pm2 on that host, nothing listening on 3000, and a `.next` carrying no `BUILD_ID`, so `next start` fails with `production-start-no-build-id` and a Playwright `webServer` there needs `npm run build` run first. Probe `.next/BUILD_ID` plus the port holder before assuming a workspace host has a running app., `/usr` 7.3G, `/var` 3.8G, `~/.9router` 1.8G, `~/.local`+`~/go`+`~/.nvm` ~2.2G. `~/.nvm` holds only **v24.19.0 (602M)** — an earlier node-22 removal did work, the disk simply refilled. Note the two virtualenvs and the `tools/` payload live *inside* the hermes install, so any "safe to delete" judgement there needs a blast-radius caveat. Confidence: 0.7
- Deploying a rebuilt node-agent to the Mac: the `darwin/arm64` build is cross-compiled on the Linux host (`GOOS=darwin GOARCH=arm64 CGO_ENABLED=0 go build -o dist/node-agent-darwin-arm64 ./cmd/agent`, or `scripts/install-mac.sh`) since the arches differ. The live binary on the Mac is `~/.hermes/bin/node-agent` — `~/apps/node-agent` does not exist there. Then back up the existing binary with a timestamp suffix first, `scp` the build, `chmod +x`, `xattr -c` (quarantine), and `launchctl kickstart -k`. Because a bad build takes the whole agent process down, always check `~/.hermes/node-agent.err.log` for `fatal error`/`panic` afterwards — truncate the log before a soak run so historical panics don't mask new ones. Confidence: 0.85
- launchd jobs on the Mac do not inherit the user login PATH: `which cmd` fails over a plain non-login `ssh` *and* inside the `com.adit.node-agent` job, so a homebrew-installed `cmd` and `node` are invisible. An empty `versions.commandcode` string in the heartbeat is a PATH bug, not a missing install — probe with `zsh -lc` (or absolute `/opt/homebrew/bin/...` paths) before concluding the CLI is absent. The mechanism: `/opt/homebrew/bin/cmd` is a **symlink to a `.mjs` file with a `#!/usr/bin/env node` shebang**, so the worker's minimal PATH makes both the version probe and every run fail with `env: node: No such file or directory` even though the binary is present. The fix is a widened `PATH=/opt/homebrew/bin:/usr/local/bin:$PATH` env applied to **all three** sites — the spawn, the `--version` probe, and the JSON-fallback retry path (mirroring the existing `dshCommandEnv`); fixing only the run leaves the heartbeat still reporting a broken version, and covering only the first two leaves the fallback path broken. This was live-broken on the Mac before it was found; `findBin` already checks `/opt/homebrew/bin`, so the binary resolves fine and the *interpreter* is what's missing. Confidence: 0.8
- **npm `.cmd` shims under a space-containing path cannot be launched from Go at all.** `C:\Program Files\nodejs\cmdc.cmd` is what `exec.LookPath` returns (via PATHEXT), and no quoting scheme survives it once any argument contains a space — which every Kanban prompt does. Three separate "fixes" were built, deployed, and proven wrong in sequence: wrapping in `cmd /c` with a quoted path, `SysProcAttr.CmdLine` (needed because `os/exec` re-escapes `"` in argv into `\"`, but cmd then splits on the space anyway), and flattening embedded newlines (the shim ends in `title %COMSPEC% & ... %*`, so a newline does end the line early — a real but secondary defect). The extensionless sibling `cmdc` is a 423-byte `#!/bin/sh` script, not a Windows executable, so preferring it doesn't help. The working fix is to **skip the shim entirely**: resolve `<shim-dir>/node_modules/<pkg>/dist/index.mjs` and run `node <that file> <args>`. Two traps in that: use the `node.exe` sitting **beside** the shim, not `findBin("node")`, because PATH resolution lands on **Volta's** shim which then hands the CLI an empty query (`No query provided`); and map the shim alias to the real package name (`cmdc` → `command-code`) when scanning `node_modules`. Verified live: `EXIT=0` with a session id. Confirmed again the same day on the dsh side: `node` on Windows PATH resolves to **Volta's** `C:\Program Files\Volta\node.exe`, not the one in `C:\Program Files\nodejs`, while npm's `dsh.cmd` still works because it invokes its sibling `node.exe` by relative path — so anything the worker reaches through `LookPath("node")` lands on Volta. **Volta is still installed there** and was first on PATH; the current mitigation is only to prepend `C:\Program Files\nodejs` ahead of it, so the same version-masking recurs if Volta's PATH entry ever moves ahead again. Removing Volta is still an open decision. Confidence: 0.9
- Redeploying the node-agent to **Windows** needs a five-step dance, because the naive one silently does nothing: (1) `scp` the new build to a **staging** name (`C:\Users\User\node-agent.new.exe`), (2) kill the **supervisor** PowerShell process — the `while ($true)` loop relaunches the agent every 3s, so `Stop-Process node-agent` alone leaves the file locked and the agent back before you look, (3) `schtasks /End /TN NodeAgent` then `Stop-Process node-agent`, (4) `Move-Item -Force` staging → `C:\Users\User\.hermes\bin\node-agent.exe`, (5) rewrite the supervisor wrapper and `schtasks /Create /Run`. The critical part: **running `install-windows.ps1` after a manual `scp` overwrites your copy** with the binary it re-downloads, and its `Move-Item` fails with an `IOException` on the locked file while the script continues anyway — so the old binary keeps serving and the change appears to have no effect. Proof is `(Get-FileHash … -Algorithm MD5).Hash` against the VPS `md5sum` **plus** `LastWriteTime` on the host; two rounds of debugging were wasted because the host timestamp was an hour behind dist. Confidence: 0.8
- The shell you land in over `ssh windows-tailscale` is **bash, not cmd** — `type "C:\Program Files\..."` returns "The network path was not found" and single-quoted PowerShell filters get mangled. Fighting backticks and nested quotes through the ssh boundary burns many round-trips. The reliable form is: write the script to a local file with `write_file`, `scp` it as `C:/Users/User/x.ps1`, then `ssh … 'powershell -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File C:/Users/User/x.ps1'` (forward slashes in the `-File` path; backslashes get eaten). Delete the script from the host afterwards. This is also how a Go/PowerShell reproduction of the exact failing invocation gets run there — building a throwaway `.exe` with `GOOS=windows` and scp'ing it is faster and more faithful than approximating the call through PowerShell. Confidence: 0.8
- Per-node harness CLI version skew is a real deployment hazard: the Mac was on Command Code 0.52.0 (no `--output-format`, no `--resume`) while the Linux host was on 1.66.0, so the exact same worker args passed locally and died on the Mac with `too many arguments`. Check each node's reported `versions.*` before trusting an E2E that only ran on one host, and `cmd --help` on the target to confirm the flag contract rather than inheriting the local build's. That `versions.*` field is also **noisy on Windows**: the version probe captures stdout+stderr together, so node's `ExperimentalWarning` on stderr lands in the string and the reported version is a warning instead of `1.66.0` — cosmetic, but it means a messy version on Windows is a known artifact rather than fresh skew, and a one-line stderr filter tidies it. Confidence: 0.8
- That Mac skew is now **resolved** — the mac node reports `commandcode 1.66.0`, matching the Linux host, so the `--output-format`/`--resume` contract holds on both worker hosts and the mac 3-loop pass is the first run on a build that actually supports the flag (0.52.0 is what died with `too many arguments`). The lesson the resolution exposed: a card left green from a previous day is **not** current evidence once anything underneath it has moved — compare the board DB mtime against the run and read the node's `versions.*` before calling a feature verified on that host. Confidence: 0.8

- node-agent's `DispatchRequest` (`cmd/agent/main.go`) already carries `Executor`, `ExecutionMode`, `Command` and `NoRTK` — `shell` + `direct` + `NoRTK:true` executes the command **verbatim** through `bash -lc` on unix and `cmd /c` on Windows (around `main.go:612`), so a shell-dependent feature needs no worker rebuild or redeploy. Two consequences for the control plane: (a) `resolvedEnv` is only populated from `os.Environ()` when preflight is on, so an explicit env block on a shell job is not a reliable channel; (b) there is **no generic env passthrough** (`NODE_AGENT_ENV`/`ExtraEnv`/`job.Env` don't exist), which is why cross-shell settings have to travel as CLI flags rather than fighting for an env seam. Confidence: 0.8

A "module not found" immediately after a branch switch is an install-staleness problem, not a missing-dependency problem — check the lockfile (`grep -c <pkg> pnpm-lock.yaml`, `ls node_modules | wc -l`) before reaching for `pnpm add`, and reconcile with `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile`, which fixes the tree without touching the lockfile when the lockfile was already correct. A **reverse-ordered query paired with a forward-walking reducer** is a class of bug no type system catches; when a report says "the panel shows the wrong thing", compare the query's ORDER BY against the reducer's loop direction before assuming the data is bad. Read the `/api/results/<id>` body raw (and un-escape the `\r\n` yourself) when you need the output off a Windows node, and say so in the report rather than presenting it as a parse bug in your own code. Two bites in one session on the node-agent server, and five more at the end of the screenshot pass — where the *shell's own command line* contained the literal pattern text, so `pgrep -f chrome-headless-shell` reported "chrome STILL" three times in a row even after `pkill -9` had already killed everything, and even sent `pkill -9 -f "vite"` at the wrapper. The fix that settles it: anchor on the **full path** (`pgrep -a -f 'chrome-headless-shell-linux64/chrome-headless-shell'`) or read `ps -eo pid,comm,args --no-headers | grep -E '<pattern>' | grep -v grep`, and confirm what a PID actually is before killing it. Corroborate with the thing that can't lie about its own name — `curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" --max-time 3 http://localhost:5173/` for a dev server, a listening-port check for a socket — because a process list that includes your own query is not evidence a process survives. Confidence: 0.85

There is **no build script at all** in this repo — the build line above is typed by hand every time, which is what makes stale-binary bugs recur. `./ctl.sh` has `build`, `build-mac`, and `build-windows` (the latter two cross-compile into `dist/`), and the server's `/dl` endpoint reads `dist/` **on every request** — so worker binaries can be rebuilt and redeployed without touching the running server, while the server binary itself still needs `./ctl.sh build` plus a restart. Caveat: `./ctl.sh status` reported the server stopped while PID 576626 was in fact serving :8788/:8789 (it was never in ctl.sh's pidfile), so confirm the process with `ss -ltnp` and `/proc/<pid>/cwd` before trusting either the status output or a `kill`. node-agent → `./ctl.sh build` then `./ctl.sh restart`, or for a live E2E `go build -o node-agent-server ./cmd/server` and `go build -o agent ./cmd/agent` then restart **both**. An agent can also run ad hoc against the local server with `NODE_AGENT_SERVER=http://127.0.0.1:8788 NODE_AGENT_ID=<id> NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=$(grep -oP '(?<=^NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=).*' ~/.hermes/node-agent.env) ./agent`. On the Mac the node-agent is a launchd agent `com.adit.node-agent` with KeepAlive, so `launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.adit.node-agent` restarts it and forces workspace re-registration. Confidence: 0.8
- **A pm2 deploy is scoped to the named app, and it isn't done until the *served* app answers.** The sequence that settles a "build it and start pm2" ask: `pm2 list` → `pm2 describe <app>` (script path, exec cwd, which binary it runs) → `pm2 env <id>` to read what the process actually has — which is how you learn **no env vars need passing**, because a bare `pm2 restart <name>` inherits the app's stored environment rather than the shell's → build (`go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server`) → `pm2 restart <app>`. Restarting only the named entry matters: this box also runs `9router` and other entries unrelated to the deploy, so `pm2 restart all` would bounce services you were never asked to touch — name the ones you left running in the close-out. The gate is live HTTP rather than the supervisor's status line: `/` → 200 whose HTML references the **new** hashed assets (`index-<hash>.js` / `index-<hash>.css`), an `/api/…` route → 401 (session gate up, route exists), and `pm2 logs <app> --lines 30 --nostream` for a clean startup. Quote the pid, the port and the asset hashes. Two additions from the `start pm2` pass: the documented **rollout order in README's deploy section** (`go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server` then `pnpm build` in `web/`) is the authoritative build sequence — read it rather than reconstructing one; and this box's `9router` gateway was **already stopped** alongside every other pm2 service, with the README flagging it as never-stop, so surface that as pre-existing state needing a separate start instead of quietly leaving the gateway down or bouncing it unasked. Confidence: 0.8
- `~/.hermes/workspaces.json` exists in two places (VPS and Mac) and can drift: the Mac's copy is what node-agent actually routes on, so sync it rather than trusting the VPS copy. Confidence: 0.75
- node-agent prefixes every result `output` with a `provenance executor=... args=[...]` line that echoes the *entire* command, including any literal sentinel strings the command contains (e.g. `printf '__STAT__\n'`). Any parser that finds its marker with `strings.Index`/first-occurrence will parse that echo instead of the real output — anchor sentinels on whole lines and skip the preamble. Confidence: 0.75
- VPS↔Mac traffic runs over a Tailscale **DERP relay (DERP(sin)), not a direct path** — ~32ms ping, ~65ms per HTTP round trip, so every blocking agent→server call has real cost. Check `tailscale ping` / `tailscale netcheck` before blaming code for latency. Confidence: 0.7
- node-agent per shell job used to pay ~7 blocking `postJSON` round trips (4 `emit` progress events + busy/idle heartbeats + result) plus a mandatory 400ms wait in `streamCommand` (loop exits only when `pending == nil`, cleared solely by the 400ms ticker): ~1.65s wall for ~60-80ms of real git work. Fixed via an ordered async `eventEmitter` (queue + single drainer, `Close()` drains before the result post, events batched not dropped) and exiting the stream loop on closure with a synchronous final flush — now ~1.0s. The residual ~1s is the DERP relay, not code. Confidence: 0.75
- The VPS root disk routinely sits at **100%** (300-400 MB free) on a volume that measured ~20GB in an early session and **~40G** at a later audit, which blocks cross-compiles. `go clean -cache` frees the ~1.2GB `~/.cache/go-build` and the build then works, but the cache refills on the next build, so check `df -h /` *before* a cross-compile and report the headroom again afterwards — a build that "succeeded" may leave the box one command away from failing. Confidence: 0.7
- **Disk-space audit recipe for the VPS.** "Why is storage almost full" is answered with evidence, not a guess: `df -h / /home` → `du -xh -d1` top level → drill into the largest dir → reconcile `du -xsh /` against `df` (they matched at 36G vs 37G, i.e. no leak) → then hunt reclaimable candidates by class. Use `-x` throughout so other mounts don't distort the numbers, and pipe through `sort -rh | head -N` to keep output readable. Two probes worth running every time: `lsof -nP | awk '/deleted/ {s+=$8}'` for **deleted-but-open files** (the usual "mystery space" culprit — here 0.5 MB total, proving the space is real), and `sudo -n tune2fs -l /dev/vda2 | grep -i reserved` because **ext4 reserves ~5% for root** (445,577 blocks ≈ 1.7G here), which is why the user sees far less free space than the totals imply. Reclaimable classes to check, in order: `~/.cache/go-build` (220M, regenerable and it refills), stale `node_modules` for apps absent from `pm2 list`, stale `.next`/`dist`/`.turbo` build outputs, deleted-but-still-registered git worktrees (`git worktree list --porcelain | grep -B1 prunable` — a *prunable* entry whose directory is already gone costs only metadata), npm/pnpm caches, and a full `.git` in an install-only clone. Confidence: 0.75
- **Disk-heavy locations on the VPS** (measured at the 40G audit): `~/.hermes` **7.7G** is the single biggest tree — of which `hermes-agent/.git` is **1.4G** (a full clone of `NousResearch/hermes-agent` kept only so the agent can be updated; the largest single reclaimable item, and any change to it touches the *running* agent), `hermes-agent/tools/` is **1.6G** (ffmpeg 512M, python 401M, chromium 364M, node 227M — in use, don't touch), and `hermes-agent/venv` + `.venv` are **813M** for what looks like a duplicate pair in one repo. Elsewhere: `~/apps` 6.8G (mostly `node_modules` — next-portfolio-blog 1.5G, cpns 1.1G, habbit 793M — plus `.next` at 553M and 410M). **Caveat learned during the cleanup: `next-portfolio-blog` is LIVE under pm2** (`pm2 describe` shows `next` running with its exec cwd there, and its 410M `.next` *is* the serving build) — never treat "stale `.next` build output" as a free win without a per-app liveness check; only `cpns-preparation/.next` (553M) turned out to be genuinely dead. The same repo on the **Mac** is the opposite state — no pm2 on that host, nothing listening on 3000, and a `.next` carrying no `BUILD_ID`, so `next start` fails with `production-start-no-build-id` and a Playwright `webServer` there needs `npm run build` run first. Probe `.next/BUILD_ID` plus the port holder before assuming a workspace host has a running app., `/usr` 7.3G, `/var` 3.8G, `~/.9router` 1.8G, `~/.local`+`~/go`+`~/.nvm` ~2.2G. `~/.nvm` holds only **v24.19.0 (602M)** — an earlier node-22 removal did work, the disk simply refilled. Note the two virtualenvs and the `tools/` payload live *inside* the hermes install, so any "safe to delete" judgement there needs a blast-radius caveat. Confidence: 0.7
- Deploying a rebuilt node-agent to the Mac: the `darwin/arm64` build is cross-compiled on the Linux host (`GOOS=darwin GOARCH=arm64 CGO_ENABLED=0 go build -o dist/node-agent-darwin-arm64 ./cmd/agent`, or `scripts/install-mac.sh`) since the arches differ. The live binary on the Mac is `~/.hermes/bin/node-agent` — `~/apps/node-agent` does not exist there. Then back up the existing binary with a timestamp suffix first, `scp` the build, `chmod +x`, `xattr -c` (quarantine), and `launchctl kickstart -k`. Because a bad build takes the whole agent process down, always check `~/.hermes/node-agent.err.log` for `fatal error`/`panic` afterwards — truncate the log before a soak run so historical panics don't mask new ones. Confidence: 0.85
- launchd jobs on the Mac do not inherit the user login PATH: `which cmd` fails over a plain non-login `ssh` *and* inside the `com.adit.node-agent` job, so a homebrew-installed `cmd` and `node` are invisible. An empty `versions.commandcode` string in the heartbeat is a PATH bug, not a missing install — probe with `zsh -lc` (or absolute `/opt/homebrew/bin/...` paths) before concluding the CLI is absent. The mechanism: `/opt/homebrew/bin/cmd` is a **symlink to a `.mjs` file with a `#!/usr/bin/env node` shebang**, so the worker's minimal PATH makes both the version probe and every run fail with `env: node: No such file or directory` even though the binary is present. The fix is a widened `PATH=/opt/homebrew/bin:/usr/local/bin:$PATH` env applied to **all three** sites — the spawn, the `--version` probe, and the JSON-fallback retry path (mirroring the existing `dshCommandEnv`); fixing only the run leaves the heartbeat still reporting a broken version, and covering only the first two leaves the fallback path broken. This was live-broken on the Mac before it was found; `findBin` already checks `/opt/homebrew/bin`, so the binary resolves fine and the *interpreter* is what's missing. Confidence: 0.8
- **npm `.cmd` shims under a space-containing path cannot be launched from Go at all.** `C:\Program Files\nodejs\cmdc.cmd` is what `exec.LookPath` returns (via PATHEXT), and no quoting scheme survives it once any argument contains a space — which every Kanban prompt does. Three separate "fixes" were built, deployed, and proven wrong in sequence: wrapping in `cmd /c` with a quoted path, `SysProcAttr.CmdLine` (needed because `os/exec` re-escapes `"` in argv into `\"`, but cmd then splits on the space anyway), and flattening embedded newlines (the shim ends in `title %COMSPEC% & ... %*`, so a newline does end the line early — a real but secondary defect). The extensionless sibling `cmdc` is a 423-byte `#!/bin/sh` script, not a Windows executable, so preferring it doesn't help. The working fix is to **skip the shim entirely**: resolve `<shim-dir>/node_modules/<pkg>/dist/index.mjs` and run `node <that file> <args>`. Two traps in that: use the `node.exe` sitting **beside** the shim, not `findBin("node")`, because PATH resolution lands on **Volta's** shim which then hands the CLI an empty query (`No query provided`); and map the shim alias to the real package name (`cmdc` → `command-code`) when scanning `node_modules`. Verified live: `EXIT=0` with a session id. Confirmed again the same day on the dsh side: `node` on Windows PATH resolves to **Volta's** `C:\Program Files\Volta\node.exe`, not the one in `C:\Program Files\nodejs`, while npm's `dsh.cmd` still works because it invokes its sibling `node.exe` by relative path — so anything the worker reaches through `LookPath("node")` lands on Volta. **Volta is still installed there** and was first on PATH; the current mitigation is only to prepend `C:\Program Files\nodejs` ahead of it, so the same version-masking recurs if Volta's PATH entry ever moves ahead again. Removing Volta is still an open decision. Confidence: 0.9
- Redeploying the node-agent to **Windows** needs a five-step dance, because the naive one silently does nothing: (1) `scp` the new build to a **staging** name (`C:\Users\User\node-agent.new.exe`), (2) kill the **supervisor** PowerShell process — the `while ($true)` loop relaunches the agent every 3s, so `Stop-Process node-agent` alone leaves the file locked and the agent back before you look, (3) `schtasks /End /TN NodeAgent` then `Stop-Process node-agent`, (4) `Move-Item -Force` staging → `C:\Users\User\.hermes\bin\node-agent.exe`, (5) rewrite the supervisor wrapper and `schtasks /Create /Run`. The critical part: **running `install-windows.ps1` after a manual `scp` overwrites your copy** with the binary it re-downloads, and its `Move-Item` fails with an `IOException` on the locked file while the script continues anyway — so the old binary keeps serving and the change appears to have no effect. Proof is `(Get-FileHash … -Algorithm MD5).Hash` against the VPS `md5sum` **plus** `LastWriteTime` on the host; two rounds of debugging were wasted because the host timestamp was an hour behind dist. Confidence: 0.8
- The shell you land in over `ssh windows-tailscale` is **bash, not cmd** — `type "C:\Program Files\..."` returns "The network path was not found" and single-quoted PowerShell filters get mangled. Fighting backticks and nested quotes through the ssh boundary burns many round-trips. The reliable form is: write the script to a local file with `write_file`, `scp` it as `C:/Users/User/x.ps1`, then `ssh … 'powershell -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File C:/Users/User/x.ps1'` (forward slashes in the `-File` path; backslashes get eaten). Delete the script from the host afterwards. This is also how a Go/PowerShell reproduction of the exact failing invocation gets run there — building a throwaway `.exe` with `GOOS=windows` and scp'ing it is faster and more faithful than approximating the call through PowerShell. Confidence: 0.8
- Per-node harness CLI version skew is a real deployment hazard: the Mac was on Command Code 0.52.0 (no `--output-format`, no `--resume`) while the Linux host was on 1.66.0, so the exact same worker args passed locally and died on the Mac with `too many arguments`. Check each node's reported `versions.*` before trusting an E2E that only ran on one host, and `cmd --help` on the target to confirm the flag contract rather than inheriting the local build's. That `versions.*` field is also **noisy on Windows**: the version probe captures stdout+stderr together, so node's `ExperimentalWarning` on stderr lands in the string and the reported version is a warning instead of `1.66.0` — cosmetic, but it means a messy version on Windows is a known artifact rather than fresh skew, and a one-line stderr filter tidies it. Confidence: 0.8
- That Mac skew is now **resolved** — the mac node reports `commandcode 1.66.0`, matching the Linux host, so the `--output-format`/`--resume` contract holds on both worker hosts and the mac 3-loop pass is the first run on a build that actually supports the flag (0.52.0 is what died with `too many arguments`). The lesson the resolution exposed: a card left green from a previous day is **not** current evidence once anything underneath it has moved — compare the board DB mtime against the run and read the node's `versions.*` before calling a feature verified on that host. Confidence: 0.8

Two consequences for the control plane: (a) `resolvedEnv` is only populated from `os.Environ()` when preflight is on, so an explicit env block on a shell job is not a reliable channel; (b) there is **no generic env passthrough** (`NODE_AGENT_ENV`/`ExtraEnv`/`job.Env` don't exist), which is why cross-shell settings have to travel as CLI flags rather than fighting for an env seam. - A **new board is directory-scanned**, so creating `~/.hermes/kanban/boards/<slug>/` with a `board.json` + `kanban.db` is enough to register it with the server and the remote dispatcher. But **never hand-write a minimal schema for it**: the live tables carry columns the dispatcher writes and a hand-rolled one does not (`task_events.run_id`/`.kind`/`.payload`, `harness_bindings.harness_kind`, `tasks.omp_session_id`/`workspace_transport`/`workspace_ssh_target`), and a missing one fails at the **finalize** step — *after* the worker already returned `success=true` — so the card sits in `running` forever and looks like a hung product. Clone instead: read `SELECT type,name,sql FROM sqlite_master WHERE sql IS NOT NULL AND name NOT LIKE 'sqlite_%'` from a busy live board (`~/.hermes/kanban/boards/f8-saas/kanban.db`), replay the DDL into the fresh DB, then `ALTER TABLE … ADD COLUMN` only for what the donor predates, and verify with `PRAGMA table_info(...)` per table. The only place that error is visible is `~/.pm2/logs/kanban-board-error.log` — `kanban-board-out.log` reads 0 bytes and sends you the wrong way. Confidence: 0.85
- A **finalize failure leaves the card stuck in `running` with no recovery and no visible error on the board** — the worker succeeded, so every signal the agent watches (node-agent's `success=true`, the `/health` capability list) reports success while the card never leaves `running`. Any auxiliary lifecycle (e2e runs, hooks, sweeps) needs its **own table plus its own event kind** — `insertEventTx(tx, taskID, kind, payload)` takes arbitrary kinds, which slots in with zero migration and is already swept by `ClearExecutionHistory`. `DispatchRemoteRaw` in `internal/kanban/nodeagent.go` passes `persistTask=false`, which skips `ClaimTaskRun` entirely: no status write, no dispatcher interference, no run-fence needed — that is the seam for work that runs on a workspace host without owning a task. The server never runs an executor locally; everything goes out over the node-agent HTTP channel. Also note `AddComment` (`comments.go:112`) auto-requeues a `review` task back to `todo`, so machine-written results on a card must use their own transaction, never the comment API. Two bites in one session on the node-agent server, and five more at the end of the screenshot pass — where the *shell's own command line* contained the literal pattern text, so `pgrep -f chrome-headless-shell` reported "chrome STILL" three times in a row even after `pkill -9` had already killed everything, and even sent `pkill -9 -f "vite"` at the wrapper. Corroborate with the thing that can't lie about its own name — `curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" --max-time 3 http://localhost:5173/` for a dev server, a listening-port check for a socket — because a process list that includes your own query is not evidence a process survives. Confidence: 0.85

Fixed via an ordered async `eventEmitter` (queue + single drainer, `Close()` drains before the result post, events batched not dropped) and exiting the stream loop on closure with a synchronous final flush — now ~1.0s. The residual ~1s is the DERP relay, not code. Confidence: 0.75

Confidence: 0.7
- **Disk-space audit recipe for the VPS.** "Why is storage almost full" is answered with evidence, not a guess: `df -h / /home` → `du -xh -d1` top level → drill into the largest dir → reconcile `du -xsh /` against `df` (they matched at 36G vs 37G, i.e. no leak) → then hunt reclaimable candidates by class. Use `-x` throughout so other mounts don't distort the numbers, and pipe through `sort -rh | head -N` to keep output readable. Two probes worth running every time: `lsof -nP | awk '/deleted/ {s+=$8}'` for **deleted-but-open files** (the usual "mystery space" culprit — here 0.5 MB total, proving the space is real), and `sudo -n tune2fs -l /dev/vda2 | grep -i reserved` because **ext4 reserves ~5% for root** (445,577 blocks ≈ 1.7G here), which is why the user sees far less free space than the totals imply. Reclaimable classes to check, in order: `~/.cache/go-build` (220M, regenerable and it refills), stale `node_modules` for apps absent from `pm2 list`, stale `.next`/`dist`/`.turbo` build outputs, deleted-but-still-registered git worktrees (`git worktree list --porcelain | grep -B1 prunable` — a *prunable* entry whose directory is already gone costs only metadata), npm/pnpm caches, and a full `.git` in an install-only clone. Confidence: 0.75
- **Disk-heavy locations on the VPS** (measured at the 40G audit): `~/.hermes` **7.7G** is the single biggest tree — of which `hermes-agent/.git` is **1.4G** (a full clone of `NousResearch/hermes-agent` kept only so the agent can be updated; the largest single reclaimable item, and any change to it touches the *running* agent), `hermes-agent/tools/` is **1.6G** (ffmpeg 512M, python 401M, chromium 364M, node 227M — in use, don't touch), and `hermes-agent/venv` + `.venv` are **813M** for what looks like a duplicate pair in one repo. Elsewhere: `~/apps` 6.8G (mostly `node_modules` — next-portfolio-blog 1.5G, cpns 1.1G, habbit 793M — plus `.next` at 553M and 410M). **Caveat learned during the cleanup: `next-portfolio-blog` is LIVE under pm2** (`pm2 describe` shows `next` running with its exec cwd there, and its 410M `.next` *is* the serving build) — never treat "stale `.next` build output" as a free win without a per-app liveness check; only `cpns-preparation/.next` (553M) turned out to be genuinely dead. The same repo on the **Mac** is the opposite state — no pm2 on that host, nothing listening on 3000, and a `.next` carrying no `BUILD_ID`, so `next start` fails with `production-start-no-build-id` and a Playwright `webServer` there needs `npm run build` run first. Probe `.next/BUILD_ID` plus the port holder before assuming a workspace host has a running app., `/usr` 7.3G, `/var` 3.8G, `~/.9router` 1.8G, `~/.local`+`~/go`+`~/.nvm` ~2.2G. `~/.nvm` holds only **v24.19.0 (602M)** — an earlier node-22 removal did work, the disk simply refilled. Note the two virtualenvs and the `tools/` payload live *inside* the hermes install, so any "safe to delete" judgement there needs a blast-radius caveat. Confidence: 0.7

The live binary on the Mac is `~/.hermes/bin/node-agent` — `~/apps/node-agent` does not exist there. Then back up the existing binary with a timestamp suffix first, `scp` the build, `chmod +x`, `xattr -c` (quarantine), and `launchctl kickstart -k`. Because a bad build takes the whole agent process down, always check `~/.hermes/node-agent.err.log` for `fatal error`/`panic` afterwards — truncate the log before a soak run so historical panics don't mask new ones. Confidence: 0.85
- launchd jobs on the Mac do not inherit the user login PATH: `which cmd` fails over a plain non-login `ssh` *and* inside the `com.adit.node-agent` job, so a homebrew-installed `cmd` and `node` are invisible. An empty `versions.commandcode` string in the heartbeat is a PATH bug, not a missing install — probe with `zsh -lc` (or absolute `/opt/homebrew/bin/...` paths) before concluding the CLI is absent. The mechanism: `/opt/homebrew/bin/cmd` is a **symlink to a `.mjs` file with a `#!/usr/bin/env node` shebang**, so the worker's minimal PATH makes both the version probe and every run fail with `env: node: No such file or directory` even though the binary is present. The fix is a widened `PATH=/opt/homebrew/bin:/usr/local/bin:$PATH` env applied to **all three** sites — the spawn, the `--version` probe, and the JSON-fallback retry path (mirroring the existing `dshCommandEnv`); fixing only the run leaves the heartbeat still reporting a broken version, and covering only the first two leaves the fallback path broken. This was live-broken on the Mac before it was found; `findBin` already checks `/opt/homebrew/bin`, so the binary resolves fine and the *interpreter* is what's missing. Confidence: 0.8
- **npm `.cmd` shims under a space-containing path cannot be launched from Go at all.** `C:\Program Files\nodejs\cmdc.cmd` is what `exec.LookPath` returns (via PATHEXT), and no quoting scheme survives it once any argument contains a space — which every Kanban prompt does. Three separate "fixes" were built, deployed, and proven wrong in sequence: wrapping in `cmd /c` with a quoted path, `SysProcAttr.CmdLine` (needed because `os/exec` re-escapes `"` in argv into `\"`, but cmd then splits on the space anyway), and flattening embedded newlines (the shim ends in `title %COMSPEC% & ... %*`, so a newline does end the line early — a real but secondary defect). The extensionless sibling `cmdc` is a 423-byte `#!/bin/sh` script, not a Windows executable, so preferring it doesn't help. The working fix is to **skip the shim entirely**: resolve `<shim-dir>/node_modules/<pkg>/dist/index.mjs` and run `node <that file> <args>`. Two traps in that: use the `node.exe` sitting **beside** the shim, not `findBin("node")`, because PATH resolution lands on **Volta's** shim which then hands the CLI an empty query (`No query provided`); and map the shim alias to the real package name (`cmdc` → `command-code`) when scanning `node_modules`. Verified live: `EXIT=0` with a session id. Confirmed again the same day on the dsh side: `node` on Windows PATH resolves to **Volta's** `C:\Program Files\Volta\node.exe`, not the one in `C:\Program Files\nodejs`, while npm's `dsh.cmd` still works because it invokes its sibling `node.exe` by relative path — so anything the worker reaches through `LookPath("node")` lands on Volta. **Volta is still installed there** and was first on PATH; the current mitigation is only to prepend `C:\Program Files\nodejs` ahead of it, so the same version-masking recurs if Volta's PATH entry ever moves ahead again. Removing Volta is still an open decision. Confidence: 0.9
- Redeploying the node-agent to **Windows** needs a five-step dance, because the naive one silently does nothing: (1) `scp` the new build to a **staging** name (`C:\Users\User\node-agent.new.exe`), (2) kill the **supervisor** PowerShell process — the `while ($true)` loop relaunches the agent every 3s, so `Stop-Process node-agent` alone leaves the file locked and the agent back before you look, (3) `schtasks /End /TN NodeAgent` then `Stop-Process node-agent`, (4) `Move-Item -Force` staging → `C:\Users\User\.hermes\bin\node-agent.exe`, (5) rewrite the supervisor wrapper and `schtasks /Create /Run`. The critical part: **running `install-windows.ps1` after a manual `scp` overwrites your copy** with the binary it re-downloads, and its `Move-Item` fails with an `IOException` on the locked file while the script continues anyway — so the old binary keeps serving and the change appears to have no effect. Proof is `(Get-FileHash … -Algorithm MD5).Hash` against the VPS `md5sum` **plus** `LastWriteTime` on the host; two rounds of debugging were wasted because the host timestamp was an hour behind dist. Confidence: 0.8
- The shell you land in over `ssh windows-tailscale` is **bash, not cmd** — `type "C:\Program Files\..."` returns "The network path was not found" and single-quoted PowerShell filters get mangled. Fighting backticks and nested quotes through the ssh boundary burns many round-trips. The reliable form is: write the script to a local file with `write_file`, `scp` it as `C:/Users/User/x.ps1`, then `ssh … 'powershell -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File C:/Users/User/x.ps1'` (forward slashes in the `-File` path; backslashes get eaten). Delete the script from the host afterwards. This is also how a Go/PowerShell reproduction of the exact failing invocation gets run there — building a throwaway `.exe` with `GOOS=windows` and scp'ing it is faster and more faithful than approximating the call through PowerShell. Confidence: 0.8
- Per-node harness CLI version skew is a real deployment hazard: the Mac was on Command Code 0.52.0 (no `--output-format`, no `--resume`) while the Linux host was on 1.66.0, so the exact same worker args passed locally and died on the Mac with `too many arguments`. Check each node's reported `versions.*` before trusting an E2E that only ran on one host, and `cmd --help` on the target to confirm the flag contract rather than inheriting the local build's. That `versions.*` field is also **noisy on Windows**: the version probe captures stdout+stderr together, so node's `ExperimentalWarning` on stderr lands in the string and the reported version is a warning instead of `1.66.0` — cosmetic, but it means a messy version on Windows is a known artifact rather than fresh skew, and a one-line stderr filter tidies it. Confidence: 0.8
- That Mac skew is now **resolved** — the mac node reports `commandcode 1.66.0`, matching the Linux host, so the `--output-format`/`--resume` contract holds on both worker hosts and the mac 3-loop pass is the first run on a build that actually supports the flag (0.52.0 is what died with `too many arguments`). The lesson the resolution exposed: a card left green from a previous day is **not** current evidence once anything underneath it has moved — compare the board DB mtime against the run and read the node's `versions.*` before calling a feature verified on that host. Confidence: 0.8

The SW is registered only on https (`web/src/main.tsx:14`), so the default `KANBAN_ADDR` 127.0.0.1:8790 local path is *not* affected — check the protocol before blaming the SW. Two bites in one session on the node-agent server, and five more at the end of the screenshot pass — where the *shell's own command line* contained the literal pattern text, so `pgrep -f chrome-headless-shell` reported "chrome STILL" three times in a row even after `pkill -9` had already killed everything, and even sent `pkill -9 -f "vite"` at the wrapper. The fix that settles it: anchor on the **full path** (`pgrep -a -f 'chrome-headless-shell-linux64/chrome-headless-shell'`) or read `ps -eo pid,comm,args --no-headers | grep -E '<pattern>' | grep -v grep`, and confirm what a PID actually is before killing it. Corroborate with the thing that can't lie about its own name — `curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" --max-time 3 http://localhost:5173/` for a dev server, a listening-port check for a socket — because a process list that includes your own query is not evidence a process survives. There is **no build script at all** in this repo — the build line above is typed by hand every time, which is what makes stale-binary bugs recur. `./ctl.sh` has `build`, `build-mac`, and `build-windows` (the latter two cross-compile into `dist/`), and the server's `/dl` endpoint reads `dist/` **on every request** — so worker binaries can be rebuilt and redeployed without touching the running server, while the server binary itself still needs `./ctl.sh build` plus a restart. Caveat: `./ctl.sh status` reported the server stopped while PID 576626 was in fact serving :8788/:8789 (it was never in ctl.sh's pidfile), so confirm the process with `ss -ltnp` and `/proc/<pid>/cwd` before trusting either the status output or a `kill`. node-agent → `./ctl.sh build` then `./ctl.sh restart`, or for a live E2E `go build -o node-agent-server ./cmd/server` and `go build -o agent ./cmd/agent` then restart **both**. An agent can also run ad hoc against the local server with `NODE_AGENT_SERVER=http://127.0.0.1:8788 NODE_AGENT_ID=<id> NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=$(grep -oP '(?<=^NODE_AGENT_TOKEN=).*' ~/.hermes/node-agent.env) ./agent`. On the Mac the node-agent is a launchd agent `com.adit.node-agent` with KeepAlive, so `launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.adit.node-agent` restarts it and forces workspace re-registration. Restarting only the named entry matters: this box also runs `9router` and other entries unrelated to the deploy, so `pm2 restart all` would bounce services you were never asked to touch — name the ones you left running in the close-out. The gate is live HTTP rather than the supervisor's status line: `/` → 200 whose HTML references the **new** hashed assets (`index-<hash>.js` / `index-<hash>.css`), an `/api/…` route → 401 (session gate up, route exists), and `pm2 logs <app> --lines 30 --nostream` for a clean startup. Quote the pid, the port and the asset hashes. Two additions from the `start pm2` pass: the documented **rollout order in README's deploy section** (`go build -o bin/kanban-board ./cmd/server` then `pnpm build` in `web/`) is the authoritative build sequence — read it rather than reconstructing one; and this box's `9router` gateway was **already stopped** alongside every other pm2 service, with the README flagging it as never-stop, so surface that as pre-existing state needing a separate start instead of quietly leaving the gateway down or bouncing it unasked. Fixed via an ordered async `eventEmitter` (queue + single drainer, `Close()` drains before the result post, events batched not dropped) and exiting the stream loop on closure with a synchronous final flush — now ~1.0s. The residual ~1s is the DERP relay, not code. Confidence: 0.75

- **Disk-space audit recipe for the VPS.** "Why is storage almost full" is answered with evidence, not a guess: `df -h / /home` → `du -xh -d1` top level → drill into the largest dir → reconcile `du -xsh /` against `df` (they matched at 36G vs 37G, i.e. no leak) → then hunt reclaimable candidates by class. Use `-x` throughout so other mounts don't distort the numbers, and pipe through `sort -rh | head -N` to keep output readable. Two probes worth running every time: `lsof -nP | awk '/deleted/ {s+=$8}'` for **deleted-but-open files** (the usual "mystery space" culprit — here 0.5 MB total, proving the space is real), and `sudo -n tune2fs -l /dev/vda2 | grep -i reserved` because **ext4 reserves ~5% for root** (445,577 blocks ≈ 1.7G here), which is why the user sees far less free space than the totals imply. Reclaimable classes to check, in order: `~/.cache/go-build` (220M, regenerable and it refills), stale `node_modules` for apps absent from `pm2 list`, stale `.next`/`dist`/`.turbo` build outputs, deleted-but-still-registered git worktrees (`git worktree list --porcelain | grep -B1 prunable` — a *prunable* entry whose directory is already gone costs only metadata), npm/pnpm caches, and a full `.git` in an install-only clone. Confidence: 0.75

The same repo on the **Mac** is the opposite state — no pm2 on that host, nothing listening on 3000, and a `.next` carrying no `BUILD_ID`, so `next start` fails with `production-start-no-build-id` and a Playwright `webServer` there needs `npm run build` run first. The live binary on the Mac is `~/.hermes/bin/node-agent` — `~/apps/node-agent` does not exist there. Then back up the existing binary with a timestamp suffix first, `scp` the build, `chmod +x`, `xattr -c` (quarantine), and `launchctl kickstart -k`. Because a bad build takes the whole agent process down, always check `~/.hermes/node-agent.err.log` for `fatal error`/`panic` afterwards — truncate the log before a soak run so historical panics don't mask new ones.
